By March 22nd, Jackie was enrolled in an intensive summer program at Tanglewood, the Boston Symphony Orchestra's summer home. By March 25th, his acceptance to Juilliard's pre-college program had been fast-tracked.
"I saw him once more that spring," I say, remembering the way hope dies in increments. "May of '79. He came to the station."
He looked different. Not older—it had only been two months—but carved down to someone his parents could recognize. The rebellion had been sanded off, leaving something smooth and acceptable and utterly lifeless.
"I wanted to tell you I'm leaving," he'd said, standing in the same spot where I'd first offered him the chance to be himself. "Juilliard accepted me for senior year. It's an opportunity I can't—"
"Can't refuse," I finished.
"Yeah."
We stood there in the control room, surrounded by the music that had promised to save us all, and said nothing that mattered.
"Nick?" I finally asked.
"We don't talk. Different worlds, you know? It was a nice dream, but..."
"But America doesn't believe in nice dreams."
He laughed, but it sounded like surrender. "Patty was right, though. About the frequency. Sometimes late at night, I can still hear it. That sound we made when we were thirteen. Before we knew we weren't supposed to harmonize."
"April 1980," I say to my 2024 audience. "I heard Nick had joined the Army. Not for patriotism or career. For the GI Bill. For the chance at college that Hop Brook had denied him."
Grace Johnson called me when he shipped out. Crying. Grateful. Terrified. Her boy was trading his body for education, the American working-class bargain that never gets discussed at Tanglewood.
"And Patty?" I continue. "Patty Lally kept writing songs. Moved to Cambridge, played the folk clubs, told truths that made people uncomfortable. Last I heard—this was '85—she was teaching music to kids in Roxbury, showing them how to find frequencies that the world insisted didn't exist."
Chapter 23: The Last Frequency
"Which brings us to now," I say, checking the clock. Twenty-three minutes until WBCN dies. "October 2024, and I've got one more piece of this story to tell."
Two weeks ago, I got an email. Yeah, email—even dinosaurs like me learned to adapt. The subject line just said "The Frequency."
It was from Patty. First time I'd heard from her in fifteen years.
"Maxanne," she wrote, "I'm organizing something. Nick's construction company is doing the renovation on the old Lincoln-Sudbury music room. Jackie's in town—his father died, he's settling the estate. The timing feels like fate. Or tragedy. Or maybe they're the same thing."
She wanted me to be there. October 13th, 2024. A Sunday. The school would be empty except for construction crews and ghosts.
"I went," I tell my invisible audience. "Of course I went. When someone offers you the chance to see if frequencies can survive forty-five years of static, you go."
Nick owned Johnson & Sons Construction now—the sons being his two boys who'd never had to wonder if the lights would stay on. He looked like his father might have, if Dan Johnson had lived past thirty-five. Solid. Tired. Successful in the way that working-class kids define success—bills paid, kids fed, nobody dying in industrial accidents.
Jackie had become what his parents had crafted—a music professor at Berklee, teaching theory to kids who could afford to major in dreams. He'd married well, divorced better, and carried himself like someone who'd won by not losing too much.
They stood in that music room—renovated twice since '79, but still holding the same wrong acoustics—and looked at each other across forty-five years of different Americas.
"Patty made us play," I continue. "Dragged out three guitars from somewhere, wouldn't take no for an answer. Said something about frequencies not caring about time."
And here's the thing—when they started playing, it was terrible. Nick's fingers, thickened by decades of manual labor, stumbled on the frets. Jackie's technique was perfect but passionless, like a museum demonstration. They sounded like what they were—strangers who'd once been friends, trying to remember a language they'd been forced to forget.
But then...
"Then Patty started singing 'Common Ground,'" I whisper, because some memories require whispers. "The same song she'd sung in '79. And something shifted."
Maybe it was muscle memory. Maybe it was the ghosts in that room. Maybe it was just three people in their sixties realizing that time was running out to fix what America had broken. But they found it. For about ninety seconds, they found the frequency.
Nick's guitar simplified, found the spaces between notes where truth lives. Jackie's precision melted into something human. And when they harmonized on the chorus—"We're all standing on common ground"—I heard it. The sound of what could have been.
When they finished, the room was silent. Then Nick said what we were all thinking:
"Too late, isn't it?"
"Yeah," Jackie replied. "About forty years too late."
They packed up the guitars, shook hands like business acquaintances, and left separately. Nick to his truck. Jackie to his Tesla. Patty to her Subaru covered in Bernie stickers and lost causes.
"I stayed behind," I tell the darkness beyond the booth. "Sat in that renovated music room and thought about frequencies. About how we find them and lose them. About how America specializes in creating static between people who should be harmonizing."
On the wall, there's still a faded poster from 1979. You can barely make out the words: "Battle of the Bands." Some janitor had painted around it for forty-five years, preserving it like evidence of a crime or a miracle.
"Eleven-forty PM," I announce. "Twenty minutes left of WBCN. Twenty minutes to tell you what all this means."
I light my actual last cigarette—the pack's empty now, like the bourbon bottle, like this studio will be in twenty minutes.
"Here's what I learned in that music room," I say. "The frequencies are still there. Patty was right about that. But we've spent forty-five years building better static. Hop Brook got bridged, but by boutique shopping, not understanding. The dump became a greenhouse, but the shit just got buried deeper. Comic books became billion-dollar movies, but the kids who read them still divide into those who relate to outsiders and those who worship power."
I think about Chris Evans, Sudbury's own Captain America, playing a hero who believes in unity while the town he grew up in perfected division.
"We won that night in 1979," I continue. "All of us. For about forty-five minutes, we remembered that music could matter more than geography. That harmony was possible across any divide. That three kids who'd found the right frequency could make eight hundred people forget they were supposed to be enemies."
"And then we went home. And America reminded us why it built the divides in the first place. And we forgot. Or pretended to forget. Or remembered but decided remembering hurt too much."
The ON AIR light pulses. Eighteen minutes left.
"But here's the thing," I say, leaning into the microphone like I'm telling a secret. "Forgetting is a choice. Division is a choice. Static is a choice. We could choose differently. We could find the frequency again. We could..."
I stop. Because I'm seventy-three years old and I've run out of hope coupons. Because I've watched too many almosts become nevers. Because America is better at building brooks than bridges.
"Coming up," I whisper, "the final moments of WBCN. But first, one last song. For three kids who proved harmony was possible. For a town that chose static instead. For all of us who know the frequency exists but can't quite remember how to find it."
I reach for the cart I've been saving. The ninety-three-second demo from summer camp, 1974. Three thirteen-year-olds finding magic before the world explained why magic wasn't allowed.
Their voices fill the booth—young, hopeful, harmonizing like it's the easiest thing in the world.
Which it was.
Until we made it impossible.
Chapter 24: The Old Roads and New Static
"Eleven-forty-two PM," I rasp into the microphone, my throat feeling like I've swallowed the entire history of Massachusetts. "Eight minutes left, and I need to tell you about roads. Because every story about division is really a story about the paths we're allowed to travel."
The demo fades out, those young voices disappearing into static like they always do. Like they always will, until we build better receivers or stop broadcasting interference.
"The Boston Post Road," I continue, "runs right through Sudbury. Same route the colonial mail riders used, same path that connected isolated farms to the idea of America. Now it's Route 20, lined with strip malls and Starbucks, but underneath that asphalt is the original dream—that information could travel, that connection could overcome distance."
I think about your sister's wedding at the Old North Church, that moment when your mother's programming overcame her principles. How you stood there, reading about eternal love while tears streamed down your face because you suddenly understood that love and division could exist in the same heart, in the same family, in the same town that preached tolerance while practicing separation.
"That's what Sudbury is," I say, lighting matches now because I'm out of cigarettes, just to have something to burn. "Longfellow's Wayside Inn, where poets made America mythic. The schoolhouse where Mary's lamb followed her, breaking the rules about what belonged where. The Sudbury River that Grandmother's house overlooked, where 'over the river and through the wood' became the American song of returning home."
But which home? The one where your mother taught you about the evils of prejudice? Or the one where she tried to stop a wedding because the groom was Jewish?
"Here's what I learned that night in 1979," I continue. "Every town is really two towns. The one it tells itself it is, and the one it actually is. The Sudbury that welcomed travelers on the Post Road, and the Sudbury that made sure those travelers knew which side of Hop Brook they belonged on."
My engineer is packing up now, preparing for the format flip at midnight. In six minutes, WBCN will become "Praise 104.1," broadcasting salvation to people who've already decided who deserves saving.
"But let me tell you what happened to that picture," I say. "The one from the Town Crier, three kids sharing a trophy they didn't know how to hold."
Chapter 25: The Frequencies That Survived
"Eleven-forty-five," I announce. "Five minutes left, and one more story to tell."
Last week, after that reunion in the music room, Patty Lally sent me something. A link to a YouTube video—because that's how music travels now, through fiber optic cables instead of airwaves, likes instead of listens.
It was grainy phone footage from someone's basement. Three kids—couldn't be more than fourteen—playing music. A Black girl on bass, an Asian boy on drums, a white kid with a guitar that looked like it came from a yard sale. They were playing "Hop Brook Blues."
Not Nick's version. Not a cover. Their own interpretation, found somehow in the digital debris of the internet age. They'd taken Nick's working-class lament and turned it into something else—a song about division that refused to accept division as permanent.
"Check the comments," Patty had written.
So I did. Hundreds of them. Kids from Sudbury, from Lincoln, from Concord. Kids whose parents worked at the Whole Foods built where Friendly's used to be. Kids whose families had moved to town for the schools without knowing the history. All of them recognizing something in that forty-five-year-old song.
"My dad went to that battle of the bands. Says nothing's changed."
"Brooks still divide but we got bridges now. Real ones and metaphorical ones."
"Anyone else here from the northeast quarter? We should jam."
"Music > ZIP codes"
"Eleven-forty-seven," I whisper. "Three minutes left, and I need to tell you something about frequencies."
They don't die. They can be suppressed, distorted, drowned in static, but they don't die. Somewhere right now, in a basement in Sudbury, kids are finding the harmony that Nick and Jackie lost. Not because the divisions have healed—Hop Brook Brewing still charges twenty-four dollars for the privilege of drinking irony—but because every generation gets to decide whether to accept the static or search for the signal.
"The Boston Post Road still runs through town," I continue, "but information travels differently now. Through fiber optic cables and cell towers, through YouTube and TikTok, through kids who never knew a world where you couldn't reach across any divide if you had wifi and something to say."
I think about those comic books becoming billion-dollar movies, about Chris Evans making Captain America real for kids who need to believe that standing up for what's right matters more than which side of town you're from. About how the X-Men are still outsiders and Superman still has privilege, but now kids can argue about it in comment sections instead of cafeterias, can find their tribes beyond geography.
"Eleven-forty-nine," I say. "One minute. Time for one more song. One more chance to find the frequency."
Chapter 26: The Last Dance at the Last Chance
"But first," I say, my hand hovering over the final cart, "I need to tell you what Maxanne Sartori learned in fifty years of spinning records and watching dreams."
The static is always louder than the signal. That's not cynicism—that's physics. It takes more energy to maintain disorder than order, more effort to divide than unite, more money to build walls than bridges. America perfected the science of static, turned it into an economy, made it seem natural as breathing.
But.
"Here's the thing about frequencies," I whisper, ten seconds to midnight, the ON AIR light pulsing its final heartbeats. "They're patient. They wait. In garages and basements, in school music rooms and church halls, in every place where kids discover that harmony is possible before adults explain why it isn't."
Five seconds.
"WBCN, 104.1 Boston, signing off for the last time. This is Maxanne Sartori reminding you that the frequency is still there. You just need to—"
The red light goes dark.
Silence.
Then, from somewhere in the booth—maybe the speakers, maybe my imagination, maybe the ghost of every song ever played on this frequency—I hear it. Three voices, young and unafraid, harmonizing across time:
"Common ground, common ground, We're all standing on common ground..."
I sit in the dark booth, in the dead station, in the city that's forgotten how to listen, and I know something with absolute certainty:
Tomorrow, somewhere in Sudbury, a kid from the good side of whatever they call Hop Brook now will meet a kid from the wrong side. They'll discover they both hear music in the space between heartbeats. They'll find a garage or a basement or a corner of the internet where geography doesn't matter.
They'll make noise that sounds like music that sounds like hope.
And America will try to stop them.
And they'll do it anyway.
Because that's what kids do—they find the frequency.
Even when—especially when—we tell them it's impossible.
The equipment powers down around me, fifty-six years of rock and roll going quiet. But in that silence, I swear I hear it:
The sound of someone, somewhere, tuning up.
Getting ready.
Finding the frequency.
Again.

THE END

08.27.25 10PM new ACT 3:
Walk This Way - Act Three: The Silence
Walk This Way - Act Three: The Silence
Chapter 21: The Geography of Time
"Eleven-oh-five PM," I announce, my voice getting that late-night gravel that used to make program directors wince. "October 2024. Forty-five years since that battle, and I need to tell you what happened to those kids. Because America loves a reunion story almost as much as it loves creating the conditions that make reunions necessary."
I light what might actually be my last cigarette—the pack's almost empty, like everything else around here. The smoke curls up toward the acoustic tiles that have absorbed fifty years of nicotine and dreams.
"But first, let me tell you about Sudbury now. Because geography changes even when people don't."
The dump? That stinking, Saturday-morning democracy where dads taught kids about waste and class without meaning to? Closed in 1987 when the EPA discovered what forty years of American excess does to groundwater. Every Saturday morning, fathers and sons would drive their station wagons loaded with garbage cans, waiting in line to back up to the edge of the landfill where bulldozers pushed America's excess into neat burial mounds. The smell—rot and methane and the sweet stench of the Cavicchio pig farm next door—that was the smell of democracy. Rich and poor alike held their noses.
"Today," I continue, watching my reflection in the dark window, aged and translucent, "that dump and pig farm is Cavicchio Greenhouses. Largest wholesale nursery in New England. The family that everyone said smelled like pig shit now sells million-dollar landscaping contracts to tech executives who live in McMansions where the working-class quarters used to be."
I laugh, but it's the laugh of someone who's lived long enough to see every irony bloom into profit.
"Giuseppe Cavicchio—the man who blocked Henry Ford's auto parts factory by refusing to sell his water rights in 1923—his great-grandchildren now own 250 acres of Sudbury's most valuable land. They turned garbage and pig waste into gold. Every spring, half of Boston drives out to buy their hydrangeas and hostas from land that used to make people roll up their car windows."
The railroad lines that sliced through town—the Massachusetts Central running east-west, the Framingham and Lowell running north-south, crossing at South Sudbury station in what they called a 'diamond crossing'—they're bike paths now. The Mass Central Rail Trail and the Bruce Freeman Rail Trail. Where freight trains once refused to stop for each other, where the junction proved that paths could cross without connecting, now joggers from both sides of Hop Brook stop to catch their breath at the same restored station platform, sharing water bottles and small talk about their Fitbit stats.
"And Hop Brook itself?" I continue. "Some marketing genius started a microbrewery in 2003. Called it Hop Brook Brewing Company. Their flagship beer? 'Divided Waters Pale Ale.' Twenty-four dollars a six-pack. They sell it to hipsters who think they're buying authenticity instead of the commodification of class struggle. The label shows a covered bridge—except Hop Brook never had a covered bridge. Just that concrete span where kids from different sides would meet to fight or fuck or pretend the divide didn't matter."
But let me get back to 1979, to the weeks after the battle. Because that's when the real division happened—not with drama or confrontation, but with the slow inevitable drift of lives moving in directions that economics had predetermined.
"March twentieth, 1979," I say, stubbing out the cigarette in a coffee mug that commemorates WBCN's fortieth anniversary—back when we thought we'd have a fiftieth. "Five days after the battle. I had Nick Johnson and the boys in Studio C, using their prize: eight hours of professional recording time."
They showed up at midnight, as agreed. Four kids in their best attempt at rock star clothes—which meant their least-torn jeans and black t-shirts that had been washed for the occasion. Joey wore his father's leather jacket, too big in the shoulders. Marcus had borrowed dress shoes that clicked on the studio floor. They stood in the same studio where Aerosmith had recorded their first demos, where the J. Geils Band had found their sound, where Boston rock history had been made.
And they froze.
"It's too clean," Nick had said, running his calloused fingers along the pristine Stratocaster we'd provided—a guitar worth more than his mother's car. "It doesn't sound like us in here."
He was right. Walking the Line's authenticity came from garage acoustics, from the way sound bounced off Dan Johnson's tool pegboard, from the imperfection that made it real. The garage where grief and grease created natural reverb. In Studio C, with its foam baffling and isolation booths, they sounded like what they were—teenagers playing dress-up in a professional's world.
"We tried for six hours," I tell my dwindling audience, though I know somewhere out there, someone's listening. Someone always is. "Recorded four takes of 'Hop Brook Blues,' each one more sterile than the last. The engineer—kid from Berklee who kept talking about 'production values'—he couldn't understand why they wanted it dirtier, rawer. 'We can add distortion in post,' he kept saying, not understanding that authentic distortion comes from amplifiers held together with duct tape and hope."
By six AM, when the morning DJ was setting up in the next booth, we all knew the truth. Some music can't survive translation. Nick left the master tape on the console.
"Keep it," he'd said. "Maybe someday someone will figure out how to make it sound like it's supposed to."
I still have that tape. Sometimes late at night, I play it and hear everything that was lost—not just in the recording, but in the translation from possible to professional. It sounds like what it was: kids who'd won a prize they couldn't use, in a world that would reward them only if they agreed to stop being themselves.
I light another cigarette—apparently the pack wasn't as empty as I thought. Or maybe I'm just buying time, delaying the inevitable midnight that's rushing toward us all.
"You know what's funny?" I ask the darkness. "Sudbury's zip code is 01776. Revolutionary numbers for a town that perfected the art of maintaining the status quo."
Chapter 22: The Frequencies We Lost
"Meanwhile," I continue, pouring water from a bottle that costs more than a six-pack used to—even water's been gentrified—"Jackie Marsh was undergoing what his parents called 'redirection.'"
Dr. Marsh had moved with the efficiency of someone who'd been planning for contingencies since his son first picked up a guitar. By March 22nd, 1979—exactly one week after the Battle—Jackie was enrolled in an intensive summer program at Tanglewood, the Boston Symphony Orchestra's summer home in the Berkshires. By March 25th, his acceptance to Juilliard's pre-college program had been fast-tracked through what Dr. Marsh called "professional connections" and everyone else called money.
"I saw him once more that spring," I say, remembering the way hope dies not in explosion but evaporation. "May 10th, 1979. He came to the station."
It was raining—that cold New England rain that makes May feel like November's hangover. Jackie stood in the lobby, his hair already cut to Juilliard specifications, wearing a blazer that fit him like a costume he was still learning to inhabit. The rebellion hadn't been beaten out of him—it had been tailored away, leaving something smooth and acceptable and utterly lifeless.
He looked different. Not older—it had only been two months—but carved down to someone his parents could recognize. Like those before-and-after photos of kids who'd been to military school, except his boot camp was cultural.
"I wanted to tell you I'm leaving," he'd said, standing in the same spot where six months earlier he'd bounced on his toes, electric with the possibility of being heard. "Juilliard accepted me for senior year. Full scholarship." He paused. "My father made some calls."
"Calls," I repeated, tasting the word like copper pennies—the flavor of bought futures.
"It's an opportunity I can't refuse."
The way he said it, like he was reading from a script his parents had written. Like he'd practiced in the mirror until it sounded almost like his own idea.
"Can't or won't?" I asked.
He looked at me then, really looked, and for a second I saw the kid who'd stood in his basement making Queen sound like revolution instead of opera. "Does it matter?"
"It's the only thing that matters."
We stood there in the control room, surrounded by gold records and signed guitars, monuments to other people's success, and said nothing that mattered. The equipment hummed its sixty-cycle hum, the frequency of American electricity, constant and invisible and absolutely controlling everything.
"Nick?" I finally asked.
"We don't talk." He straightened his blazer, a gesture he'd learned from watching his father adjust his suit before faculty meetings. "Different worlds, you know? It was a nice dream, but..."
"But America doesn't believe in nice dreams."
He laughed, but it sounded like surrender performed in a minor key. "Patty was right, though. About the frequency. Sometimes late at night, I can still hear it. That sound we made at camp when we were thirteen. Before we knew we weren't supposed to harmonize."
"Everyone knows they're not supposed to harmonize," I said. "That's why harmony matters."
He left without saying goodbye, just nodded and walked out into that May rain that was washing Sudbury clean for another generation of divisions.
"April 1980," I say to my 2024 audience, though I know most of them are probably already scrolling through their phones, looking for the next thing to be angry about. "I heard Nick had joined the Army. Not for patriotism or career. For the GI Bill. For the chance at college that Hop Brook had denied him."
Grace Johnson called me when he shipped out to Fort Benning. It was six in the morning—she'd just gotten off her overnight shift at the hospital, the third double that week.
"He's going to make something of himself," she said, and I could hear her trying to convince herself. "The recruiter said after his service, he can go to any state school. Engineering, maybe. His father would have liked that."
Her boy was trading his body for education, the American working-class bargain that never gets discussed at Tanglewood or Juilliard. The recruiter didn't mention that by the time Nick got out, he'd be four years behind Jackie in the race that had been fixed from birth.
"Did he take his guitar?" I asked.
"No." A pause. "Said it wouldn't survive basic training. Said nothing real does."
"And Patty?" I continue, lighting what I swear is my last cigarette—though I've been saying that for forty-five years. "Patty Lally kept writing songs. Moved to Cambridge, played the folk clubs in Harvard Square where the academics came to feel authentic for the price of a cover charge and two-drink minimum."
She sent me a postcard in December 1980. A picture of the Old North Bridge in her hometown of Concord, where the shot heard 'round the world was fired. On the back, she'd written: "The British and Americans had to figure out how to share the same town after trying to kill each other. We're still figuring it out. Playing at Club Passim Tuesday nights. The frequency persists. - P"
I went once, in February 1981. She was playing to maybe twenty people, half of them more interested in their conversations than her music. But she sang like the room was full, like every song mattered, like someone might be listening who needed to hear that harmony was still possible.
Between sets, she told me she was teaching music to kids in Roxbury during the days. "Showing them how to find frequencies that the world insists don't exist."
"How's that working out?"
"About as well as you'd expect. But sometimes—sometimes one of them finds it. That sound between the notes where truth lives. And for about thirty seconds, they believe they matter."
She played "Common Ground" that night, slower than at the Battle, aged like whiskey in the barrel of experience. When she sang the line "We're all standing on common ground," a drunk Harvard kid laughed and said, "Tell that to my trust fund." His friends hushed him, embarrassed, but Patty just smiled and said into the microphone, "Even trust fund babies need solid ground to stand on. Money just makes you think you're floating."
"Last I heard from Patty directly," I tell the darkness, "was 1985. She was still teaching, still playing Tuesday nights, still believing that frequencies could be found by anyone willing to listen past the static."
But I kept track. The way you keep track of people who were there when history almost changed. She never made it big, never got the record deal, never became the voice of her generation. She became something else—the teacher who shows kids that their noise matters, that their frequencies are real, that harmony is possible even when America insists on dissonance.
"Eleven-fifteen," I announce. "Forty-five minutes until midnight. Until WBCN becomes just another memory in a city full of them."
Chapter 23: The Inventory of Silence (Revised)
"Which brings us to now," I say, checking the clock—11:17 PM, October 2024, forty-three minutes until WBCN dies. "I need to tell you what became of them. Not through some contrived reunion, but through the inventory of lives lived parallel but never intersecting again."
Two weeks ago, preparing for this final broadcast, I did what journalists do—I researched. Made calls. Searched the digital debris where everyone leaves traces now. Followed electronic breadcrumbs of three lives that diverged in 1979 and never reconverged.
Nick Johnson owns Johnson & Sons Construction now. The "& Sons" are his two boys—Tyler and Mason—who've never wondered if the lights would stay on, never eaten silence for dinner when the overtime didn't come through. The company specializes in historical restoration, which feels like the kind of irony that only America could produce—the working-class kid who couldn't afford history now gets paid to preserve it.
The company website shows him standing in front of the Wayside Inn after completing its latest renovation. The same inn where Henry Ford tried to preserve American authenticity by buying it. Nick's crew had to fix Ford's fixes, because even preservation needs maintenance.
Grace Johnson died in 2018. "Peacefully," the obituary said, though I wonder what peace means for someone who worked doubles her whole life. Nick was listed as her only survivor. The service was held at Martha Mary Chapel—the non-denominational chapel Ford built on the Wayside property. Even in death, geography mattered.
"Jackie Marsh," I continue, scrolling through his faculty page on the college website. "Professor of Music Theory and Composition. Teaches a course called 'The Architecture of American Popular Music.' One of his students wrote online that he's 'brilliant but seems sad, like he's listening for something in the music that isn't there anymore.'"
He married well—a violinist from the Boston Symphony—divorced better, and kept the house on the good side of what used to be Hop Brook. The Marsh family compound still exists, though Dr. Marsh died in 2022 and Mrs. Marsh lives in assisted care, unable to remember the son she so carefully sculpted.
Jackie's published papers have titles like "Harmonic Convergence in Late Twentieth Century Rock" and "The Democratic Fallacy of Garage Band Culture." Academic ways of discussing what he once lived. Like a war correspondent who becomes a military historian—safer to analyze battles than fight them.
"And Patty," I say, finding her not through professional profiles but through a poorly xeroxed flyer someone posted on a local music forum. "Still teaching. Still playing. Still frequency-hunting."
She runs a music program in Roxbury called "Finding the Frequency"—she actually used the phrase, thirty years before it became a self-help cliché. Tuesday nights, she still plays at Club Passim, though the Harvard Square she knew has been replaced by banks and chains. Her bio says she's a "veteran of the Boston music scene," which is how we describe people who survived without succeeding.
A Globe feature from 2019 called her "Boston's unsung hero of music education" and mentioned, briefly, that she'd won a Battle of the Bands in 1979. They got the year right but the story wrong, said she'd beaten "several local bands" as if specificity might hurt someone's feelings.
"The zip code thing kills me," I mutter, not really into the microphone but not exactly off-air either. "Sudbury: 01776. Those revolutionary numbers stamped on every piece of mail, every package, every official document. As if the zip code could make them patriots, as if numbers could overcome the divisions they so carefully maintained."
During the Bicentennial in 1976—three years before our Battle—Sudbury had four different Fourth of July celebrations. Not one unified parade but four separate events, one for each quadrant of town. The northeast quarter held theirs at Featherland Park, with Little League teams and a barbecue that charged five dollars a plate. The southeast had theirs at the high school, with the school band playing Sousa marches. The northwest gathered at Heritage Park, with historical reenactors and speeches about democracy. The southwest met at the town green, with food trucks that would later be called "gourmet" and a jazz quartet.
Four celebrations of the same independence, in the same town, on the same day. And nobody thought it was strange. That's how division becomes natural—when even celebration segregates itself.
The Sudbury Minute Men still march to Concord every Patriots' Day, reenacting their support of the shot heard 'round the world. Kids from all four quadrants dress in colonial garb, carry muskets that fire blanks, pretend to be unified against a common enemy. Then they march back home to their separate corners, their separate schools within schools, their separate futures that were determined by which side of Hop Brook their parents could afford.
"There's something else," I say, leaning closer to the microphone because this feels like a secret. "About those railroad lines that divided the town."
The diamond crossing where the Massachusetts Central met the Framingham and Lowell—it's a rotary now. Cars circling endlessly, each taking their predetermined exit. But if you know where to look, you can still see the ghost of those rails in the pavement. Lines that went nowhere, that carried everything, that proved you could cross paths without ever connecting.
Someone told me—this was maybe ten years ago—that on certain nights, when the conditions are just right, you can stand at that rotary and hear it. The phantom frequency of trains that no longer run, on rails that no longer exist, carrying cargo that no longer matters to destinations that have been converted to condos.
But that's probably just the kind of story people tell in towns that have run out of future and started feeding on their past.
"Eleven twenty-three," I announce. "Thirty-seven minutes left. Thirty-seven minutes to explain why any of this matters."
I don't mention that I've been checking on them through the quiet stalking we now call "keeping up with people." Construction photos where Nick always stands slightly apart. Academic talks by Jackie, brilliant but bloodless. Patty's Tuesday performances to shrinking crowds.
They don't follow each other's digital trails. Don't acknowledge each other's existence in the electronic ether where everyone pretends to be connected. The algorithms that suggest connections haven't figured out that some frequencies, once lost, are too painful to restore.
Because algorithms don't understand that some connections are severed not by distance but by proximity to what was lost. That some silences are louder than any frequency. That forty-five years isn't long enough to forget but is too long to remember without breaking.
Now, let me synthesize Chapters 24-26 from the best elements of your three versions:
Chapter 24: The Boston Post Road of Memory
"Eleven twenty-five PM," I rasp into the microphone, my throat feeling like I've swallowed forty-five years of static. "Thirty-five minutes until WBCN becomes another ghost on the Boston Post Road."
The demo from 1974 fades out—those three young voices disappearing into the ether like all voices eventually do. I reach for water but find only an empty bottle, its plastic crackling like old bones.
"The Boston Post Road," I continue, "Route 20 to the modern world, but once it carried mail by horseback from Boston to New York. Messages of revolution, of commerce, of love and loss. It runs right through Sudbury, past Longfellow's Wayside Inn where travelers rested and poets made America mythic."
Down that same road sits the little red schoolhouse where Mary's lamb followed her, breaking the rules about what belonged in school, becoming a nursery rhyme every American child learns before they learn that some things don't belong anywhere. Sarah Josepha Hale wrote that poem in 1830, but Henry Ford thought it happened at that particular schoolhouse, so he moved it to the Wayside property in 1927. Even our nursery rhymes are about displacement.
"The Sudbury River runs under that road," I tell whoever's still listening at this ungodly hour. "Lydia Maria Child wrote about it in 1844—'Over the river and through the wood, to Grandfather's house we go.' Another children's song about journeys, about connections across distance. But what Child didn't mention was that sometimes the river is too wide, the woods too dark, and grandfather's house is on the wrong side of everything."
I think about WBCN as another kind of post road. For fifty-six years, we carried messages along the airwaves. Not by horseback but by radio wave. Songs traveling from Boston to the suburbs and back, carrying news of who we were, who we might become, who we were terrified to be.
"But just like the Boston Post Road got replaced by highways, bypassed by interstates, turned into a tourist curiosity instead of a lifeline, radio is being replaced by algorithms that know what you want to hear before you do. By artificial intelligences that can predict your taste but can't recognize when a song might save your life."
The irony isn't lost on me—the Cavicchios who blocked Henry Ford's factory with their pig farm and water rights, they're the ones who won. Their greenhouses sit on land that appreciates every year, while Ford's dream of preserving authentic America became just another museum that charges admission to see a fake version of a real past.
"Here's what I learned," I say, lighting a match just to watch it burn. "Roads leave traces even when they're paved over. Rails leave ghosts even when they're turned into bike paths. Frequencies persist even when the stations go dark."
Chapter 25: The Weight of What We Carry
"Eleven thirty-five PM," I announce. "Twenty-five minutes left, and I need to tell you about weight."
Not the weight of objects—though God knows this booth is full of those, forty years of promotional CDs and band stickers and photos of people who thought they'd be famous forever. I mean the weight of what we carry. The weight of what could have been.
"Three weeks ago," I begin, "I went back to Lincoln-Sudbury. Just to look. The music room's been renovated twice since 1979. They've got digital equipment now, software that can make anyone sound professional. But the room still has the wrong acoustics—some failures are architectural."
On the wall, barely visible under forty-five years of paint, there's still the ghost of a poster. "Battle of the Bands, March 15, 1979." The janitors painted around it for decades, like it was a shrine or evidence or just too much trouble to scrape off completely.
Standing in that room, I could feel them—not the actual people Nick and Jackie and Patty became, but the kids they were that night. The potential that hung in the air like humidity before a thunderstorm. The moment before America reminded them that potential and permission are different things.
"You know what's funny about Sudbury?" I ask the darkness beyond the booth. "During the Bicentennial, when America was congratulating itself on two hundred years of democracy, the town's historical society published a book. 'Sudbury: 1639-1976.' Six hundred pages about a town six miles square. And you know what they spent exactly two paragraphs on? The Cavicchio pig farm. The dump. The entire working-class quarter north of Hop Brook got two paragraphs in six hundred pages."
But here's the thing—the Cavicchios didn't need paragraphs. They had patience. While everyone else was writing history, they were buying land. While the town was celebrating its revolutionary zip code—01776, as if numbers could make you patriotic—the Cavicchios were turning pig shit into gold.
"Eleven forty PM. Twenty minutes."
The Martha Mary Chapel—I keep wanting to call it Old North Church but that's in Concord where Patty's from, where the shot heard 'round the world was fired—Henry Ford built it in the 1920s, naming it after his mother Mary and mother-in-law Martha. He wanted a non-denominational space where anyone could worship. But even non-denominational has denominations. Even unity divides itself.
I think about your sister's wedding there, how your mother's principles collapsed when faced with actual difference. How you stood at that podium reading about eternal love while tears streamed down your face, suddenly understanding that love and division could live in the same heart, in the same family, in the same town that preached tolerance while practicing separation.
"That's what Sudbury is," I say. "A town that teaches its children about revolution while maintaining the monarchy. That celebrates the shot heard 'round the world while making sure nobody disturbs the peace. That stamps 01776 on every envelope while making sure the mail stays in its proper ZIP code."
Chapter 26: The Frequency of Silence
"Eleven fifty PM," I whisper. "Ten minutes. Time enough for one more story. One more song. One more chance to explain why any of this mattered."
I've saved this for last. The thing I've never said on air. The reason I've spent four hours telling you about three kids who found and lost harmony.
"In 1980," I say, "after Jackie had gone to Juilliard, after Nick had joined the Army, after the Battle of the Bands had become just another story about how things don't change, Patty Lally came back to the station. She had a demo tape. One song. She said it was for Nick, for Jackie, for everyone who'd been there that night."
I've never played it on air. Never played it for anyone. Kept it like evidence of a crime or a prayer or a possibility that hurt too much to share.
"It's called 'After the Battle,'" I say, sliding the cart into the machine with hands that haven't stopped shaking since ten o'clock. "And if you've stayed with me this long, through all these stories about division and frequency and the distances we maintain, then you deserve to hear how the story really ends."
Patty's voice fills the booth—older than in 1979 but still carrying that truth that made everyone listen:
"After the battle, when the smoke has cleared,When the winners are forgotten and the losers disappeared,When the trophies gather dust and the stages fall apart,That's when music matters, that's when we restart..."
Her guitar is simple—three chords and the truth, as Woody Guthrie prescribed. But the words...
"Nick's in Carolina, building bridges out of steel,Jackie's in a classroom, teaching kids what's real,I'm in Massachusetts, singing to an empty room,But the frequency that joined us still cuts through the gloom..."
"Eleven fifty-five," I whisper. "Five minutes left."
"So here's to all the battles that we thought would change our lives,Here's to all the mornings when division still survives,Here's to bridges built and burned and built again in vain,Here's to finding frequencies despite the static's pain..."
The song fades out, but Patty's voice continues—spoken, not sung:
"Maxanne—we proved it was possible. Even if just for one night. Even if the world made us forget. The frequency exists. The harmony is real. The bridges can be built. We just have to keep trying, keep playing, keep believing that music matters more than the silence between us."
"Eleven fifty-nine and thirty seconds," I announce. "Thirty seconds left."
I lean into the microphone one last time.
"This is Maxanne Sartori, signing off from WBCN, 104.1 on your FM dial. For fifty-six years, we've been the Rock of Boston. We've played the hits and the misses, the legends and the losers, the battles and the truces."
Ten seconds.
"But mostly, we've played the frequency of possibility. The sound of what could be if we just—"
Five seconds.
"—stopped choosing static—"
Two seconds.
"—over songs."
Midnight.
The ON AIR sign goes dark. The booth falls silent. WBCN is dead.
But somewhere in Sudbury, on a road that used to carry messages by horseback, in a town divided by water and wealth and waste, three kids are still harmonizing in the garage of memory. Still proving it's possible. Still waiting for the rest of us to find the frequency.
The silence stretches out. But if you listen carefully—if you know how to tune past the static—you can still hear them singing.
You can always hear them singing.
If you remember how to listen.

Act Synopses (with expansions)
ACT ONE: THE FREQUENCY
In 2024, legendary WBCN DJ Maxanne Sartori begins her final broadcast before the station dies at midnight, choosing to tell the story of two boys whose musical partnership was destroyed by class divisions in 1979 Sudbury, Massachusetts. Through flashbacks, we witness Nick Johnson (working-class, father dead from industrial accident) and Jackie Marsh (upper-middle-class, surgeon's son) as former friends now rivals, separated by Hop Brook and the town's four quadrants divided by both nature and the town dump. Their shared history at a 1974 music camp, where they harmonized with Patty Lally before the world taught them they shouldn't, haunts their present rivalry.
As the Battle of the Bands approaches, both boys struggle with their assigned roles—Nick's authentic garage rock versus Jackie's classical training forced into rock performance. The competition transforms from a simple school event into a class war, with WBCN's coverage elevating it to regional significance. Through Maxanne's visits to both boys, we see how their musical preferences (Aerosmith vs. Queen) represent deeper divisions about authenticity versus sophistication, with adults like Bob Wentworth trying to bridge gaps while others like Dr. Marsh reinforce them.
ACT TWO: THE STATIC
The Battle of the Bands unfolds in real-time, with eight bands representing different facets of Sudbury's divided community—from the working-class Jesters to the all-girl Cherry Bomb to the rich kids' Establishment. Each full performance reveals not just musical style but class position, with the audience initially segregated by geography gradually beginning to mix as music temporarily dissolves boundaries. Jackie's Crown Jewels deliver technical perfection until he breaks script with Elton John's "Your Song," revealing the emotion his parents tried to train out of him.
The competition's climax comes when Patty Lally's Rumours performs original songs about their shared past, explicitly naming the 1974 camp where "three kids found a frequency," followed by Nick's Walking the Line, whose original "Hop Brook Blues" devastates the audience with its raw truth about division. When Walking the Line wins, Nick's attempt to share the victory with Jackie fails as Dr. Marsh immediately reasserts control, forcing Jackie to choose between momentary unity and his predetermined future. The parking lot aftermath shows all divisions immediately reinstating themselves, with Maxanne observing they witnessed something "both revolutionary and completely futile."
ACT THREE: THE SILENCE
Jumping between 1979's immediate aftermath and 2024's final broadcast, we witness the slow dissolution of all possibility as Nick, Jackie, and Patty are pulled into their predetermined paths. The expanded chapters follow Nick's final summer before joining the Army for the GI Bill, Jackie's isolation at Tanglewood then Juilliard, and Patty's evolution into a Cambridge folk singer and Roxbury music teacher. The geography of Sudbury transforms—the dump becomes Cavicchio's greenhouse empire, railroad lines become bike paths, Hop Brook gets a microbrewery—but the divisions simply take new forms.
As midnight approaches, Maxanne reveals what became of each through "digital breadcrumbs": Nick owns a construction company restoring the history he couldn't afford to inhabit; Jackie teaches music theory at Berklee, academically analyzing what he once lived; Patty still seeks the frequency in Roxbury classrooms and empty Cambridge clubs. Playing Patty's never-aired 1980 recording "After the Battle," Maxanne counts down WBCN's final seconds, ending with the revelation that "somewhere in Sudbury, three kids are still harmonizing in the garage of memory," but only if we remember how to listen past the static we've chosen over songs.
Two-Page Novel Synopsis
WALK THIS WAY
On October 15, 2024, Maxanne Sartori, legendary DJ who discovered Aerosmith and launched Boston rock, prepares for WBCN's final broadcast before the station dies at midnight. At 73, she has four hours to tell one last story—not about the bands who made it, but about two boys whose musical partnership could have changed everything if America hadn't perfected the art of division.
The story unfolds in two timelines. In 2024, Maxanne broadcasts from WBCN's dying booth, weaving between on-air monologues and private revelations, counting down to midnight when the station will flip to Christian radio. In 1979, we witness the story she's telling: Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh, once inseparable friends in middle school, now seniors at Lincoln-Sudbury High, divided by Hop Brook—the stream that separates Sudbury's working class from its upper-middle elite.
Nick lives with his widowed mother Grace, who works double shifts at the hospital since his father died in a construction accident. His garage band, Walking the Line, practices among his dead father's tools, making authentic rock from grief and class rage. Jackie, son of a Harvard Medical professor and a Wellesley poet, has been trained for excellence since birth. His band, Crown Jewels, practices in a basement worth more than Nick's house, performing Queen covers with classical precision but no soul.
The novel's first act establishes Sudbury's geography of division: four quadrants separated by Hop Brook and the town dump (with its adjacent Cavicchio pig farm), by railroad lines that cross but don't connect, by parents who preach tolerance while practicing segregation. Through flashbacks, we learn Nick and Jackie once harmonized perfectly at a 1974 music camp with Patty Lally from Concord, before the world explained why working-class authenticity and upper-class education couldn't mix.
When Lincoln-Sudbury announces its Battle of the Bands for March 15, 1979, what begins as a school competition becomes a class war. WBCN's coverage, led by Maxanne herself, transforms it into a regional event. Eight bands enter, each representing different aspects of Sudbury's divisions: The Jesters (working-class Lincoln kids), Cherry Bomb (all-girl punk challenging male rock supremacy), The Establishment (rich kids' sterile precision), Dead Heads (stoner transcendence), Crown Jewels, Walking the Line, and unexpectedly, Rumours—Patty Lally's Concord band that refuses to choose sides.
The battle itself occupies the novel's entire second act, with each band receiving a full chapter for their performance. The evening progresses from segregated seating to gradual mixing as music temporarily dissolves boundaries. Jackie's technical perfection cracks when he breaks into Elton John's "Your Song," revealing the emotion his parents tried to train out of him. Patty's original songs explicitly name the shared past: "Three kids found a frequency in 1974 / Before the world explained what harmony was for." Nick's devastating original "Hop Brook Blues" tells the truth about division with such raw power that even the judges—teachers who maintain these divisions daily—can't ignore it.
When Walking the Line wins, Nick attempts to share the victory with Jackie and Patty, but Dr. Marsh's immediate intervention forces Jackie to choose between momentary unity and his predetermined future at Juilliard. The parking lot aftermath reveals all divisions immediately reasserting themselves, what Maxanne calls "both revolutionary and completely futile."
Act Three jumps between the immediate aftermath (1979-1980) and Maxanne's 2024 broadcast. Extended chapters follow the slow dissolution: Jackie's isolation at Tanglewood then Juilliard, becoming the musician his parents designed; Nick's decision to join the Army for the GI Bill, trading his body for education; Patty's evolution into a Cambridge folk singer and Roxbury music teacher, "showing kids how to find frequencies the world insists don't exist."
By 2024, the geography has transformed but divisions persist. The Cavicchio pig farmers—who once blocked Henry Ford's factory with their water rights—now own the largest greenhouse empire in New England. The dump has become their goldmine. Railroad lines are bike paths where former enemies jog together without recognizing the irony. Hop Brook has a microbrewery selling "Divided Waters Pale Ale" for $24 a six-pack. Nick owns a construction company that restores the history he couldn't afford to inhabit. Jackie teaches at Berklee, brilliantly analyzing what he once lived. Patty still plays Tuesday nights to shrinking crowds.
As midnight approaches, Maxanne plays Patty's never-aired 1980 recording "After the Battle," a song about how "the frequency that joined us still cuts through the gloom." The novel culminates with WBCN going dark at midnight, but Maxanne's final words insist the frequency persists: "somewhere in Sudbury, three kids are still harmonizing in the garage of memory," waiting for us to stop choosing static over songs.
The epilogue follows Maxanne leaving the dead station, driving through Sudbury's transformed landscape at dawn, noting every irony: Chris Evans from Sudbury playing Captain America, a hero of unity from a town that perfected division; the Martha Mary Chapel hosting weddings between faiths that families still secretly oppose; kids at the Hop Brook bridge making music on instruments their parents can barely afford while the brook itself no longer divides anything except property values.
Literary Comparisons
This novel sits in the tradition of Richard Russo's "Empire Falls" and Russell Banks's "The Sweet Hereafter"—examining how class divisions destroy American communities through a single crystallizing event. The radio station framework and musical focus echo Nick Hornby's "High Fidelity" but with deeper social critique. The dual timeline structure and meditation on lost possibility resembles Michael Chabon's "The Amazing Adventures of Kavalier & Clay" or Jennifer Egan's "A Visit from the Goon Squad".
The specific use of real historical detail within fiction follows E.L. Doctorow's "Ragtime" tradition, while the focus on teenage possibility destroyed by adult systems echoes Donna Tartt's "The Secret History". The novel's examination of how art fails to transcend class barriers despite our myths about its democratizing power connects to Colson Whitehead's "Sag Harbor" and Tom Perrotta's "The Leftovers".
Pulitzer Potential
The novel has several elements that appeal to Pulitzer committees: exploration of American class divisions through an unexpected lens, innovative structure using radio broadcast as narrative frame, and deep regionalism that speaks to universal themes. The music-as-democracy metaphor and examination of how the 1970s' promise failed could resonate with contemporary concerns about inequality.
However, Pulitzer committees typically favor either experimental literary fiction (like "The Netanyahus" by Joshua Cohen) or sweeping social novels (like "The Overstory" by Richard Powers). "Walk This Way" would need exceptional prose execution and perhaps a more ambitious scope to compete. The ending's insistence that "frequencies persist" might be seen as too hopeful for current literary tastes that favor bleaker views of American division.
The novel's strength lies in its specific regional detail and unique narrative voice. With the expanded performance chapters and deeper exploration of the aftermath, it could achieve the scope needed for major recognition. The key would be ensuring every sentence earns its place and the metaphor of frequency/static doesn't become heavy-handed.

Corrected Two-Page Novel Synopsis
WALK THIS WAY
On October 15, 2024, Maxanne Sartori, legendary DJ who discovered Aerosmith and launched Boston rock, prepares for WBCN's final broadcast before the station dies at midnight. At 73, she has four hours to tell one last story—not about the bands who made it, but about two boys whose musical partnership could have changed everything if America hadn't perfected the art of division.
The story unfolds in two timelines. In 2024, Maxanne broadcasts from WBCN's dying booth, weaving between on-air monologues and private revelations, counting down to midnight when the station will flip to Christian radio. In 1979, we witness the story she's telling: Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh, once inseparable friends in middle school, now seniors at Lincoln-Sudbury High, divided by Hop Brook—the stream that separates Sudbury's working class from its upper-middle elite.
Nick lives with his widowed mother Grace, who works double shifts at Friendly's restaurant since his father Dan died in a construction accident, crushed under steel beams when a crane cable snapped. His garage band, Walking the Line, practices among his dead father's tools, making authentic rock from grief and class rage. Jackie, son of a Boston University professor who writes about the working class without living among them, and a Wellesley-educated poet mother, has been trained for excellence since birth. His band, Crown Jewels, practices in a basement worth more than Nick's house, performing Queen covers with classical precision but no soul.
The novel's first act establishes Sudbury's geography of division: four quadrants separated by Hop Brook and the town dump (with its adjacent Cavicchio pig farm), by railroad lines that cross but don't connect, by parents who preach tolerance while practicing segregation. Through flashbacks, we learn Nick and Jackie once harmonized perfectly at a 1974 music camp with Patty Lally from Concord, before the world explained why working-class authenticity and upper-class education couldn't mix.
When Lincoln-Sudbury announces its Battle of the Bands for March 15, 1979, what begins as a school competition becomes a class war. WBCN's coverage, led by Maxanne herself, transforms it into a regional event. Eight bands enter, each representing different aspects of Sudbury's divisions: The Jesters (working-class Lincoln kids), Cherry Bomb (all-girl punk challenging male rock supremacy), The Establishment (rich kids' sterile precision), Dead Heads (stoner transcendence), Crown Jewels, Walking the Line, and unexpectedly, Rumours—Patty Lally's Concord band that refuses to choose sides.
The battle itself occupies the novel's entire second act, with each band receiving a full chapter for their performance. The evening progresses from segregated seating to gradual mixing as music temporarily dissolves boundaries. Jackie's technical perfection cracks when he breaks into Elton John's "Your Song," revealing the emotion his parents tried to train out of him. Patty's original songs explicitly name the shared past: "Three kids found a frequency in 1974 / Before the world explained what harmony was for." Nick's devastating original "Hop Brook Blues" tells the truth about division with such raw power that even the judges—teachers who maintain these divisions daily—can't ignore it.
When Walking the Line wins, Nick attempts to share the victory with Jackie and Patty, but Dr. Marsh's immediate intervention forces Jackie to choose between momentary unity and his predetermined future at Juilliard. The parking lot aftermath reveals all divisions immediately reasserting themselves, what Maxanne calls "both revolutionary and completely futile."
Act Three jumps between the immediate aftermath (1979-1980) and Maxanne's 2024 broadcast. Extended chapters follow the slow dissolution: Jackie's isolation at Tanglewood then Juilliard, becoming the musician his parents designed; Nick's decision to join the Army for the GI Bill, trading his body for education while his mother Grace continues her exhausting shifts at Friendly's; Patty's evolution into a Cambridge folk singer and Roxbury music teacher, "showing kids how to find frequencies the world insists don't exist."
By 2024, the geography has transformed but divisions persist. The Cavicchio pig farmers—who once blocked Henry Ford's factory with their water rights—now own the largest greenhouse empire in New England. The dump has become their goldmine. Railroad lines are bike paths where former enemies jog together without recognizing the irony. Hop Brook has a microbrewery selling "Divided Waters Pale Ale" for $24 a six-pack. Nick owns a construction company that restores the history he couldn't afford to inhabit. Jackie teaches at Berklee, brilliantly analyzing what he once lived. Patty still plays Tuesday nights to shrinking crowds.
As midnight approaches, Maxanne plays Patty's never-aired 1980 recording "After the Battle," a song about how "the frequency that joined us still cuts through the gloom." The novel culminates with WBCN going dark at midnight, but Maxanne's final words insist the frequency persists: "somewhere in Sudbury, three kids are still harmonizing in the garage of memory," waiting for us to stop choosing static over songs.
The epilogue follows Maxanne leaving the dead station, driving through Sudbury's transformed landscape at dawn, noting every irony: Chris Evans from Sudbury playing Captain America, a hero of unity from a town that perfected division; the Martha Mary Chapel hosting weddings between faiths that families still secretly oppose; kids at the Hop Brook bridge making music on instruments their parents can barely afford while the brook itself no longer divides anything except property values.

Regarding Bruce Springsteen and Maxanne: This is a powerful historical detail that could add authenticity. The 1972/73 timeline works perfectly - Springsteen was completely unknown then, playing small venues, trying to break through. Having Maxanne conduct his first radio interview and him performing "Rosalita" (from his second album, "The Wild, The Innocent & The E Street Shuffle") shows her eye for talent before fame.
You could weave this into Chapter 1 or 2 where Maxanne establishes her credibility: "I gave Springsteen his first radio interview in '73 when he was just another Jersey kid with a guitar and something to prove. He played 'Rosalita' live in Studio C, just him and Clarence on sax, and I knew—the same way I knew with Tyler, the same way I knew with those three kids in '74—that the frequency was there."
This detail reinforces that Maxanne recognizes authentic talent regardless of its packaging, which makes her investment in Nick and Jackie more meaningful. It also shows WBCN's role in launching careers before Clear Channel homogenized everything.
